# Draazy E2E — Coverage Traceability Matrix

Audit of every app route / feature (`frontend/src/App.jsx` + `docs/flows/`) against the
Playwright specs in `tests/`. Status legend:

- ✅ **Covered** — a dedicated spec exercises the feature's key buttons/functions.
- 🟡 **Partial** — touched incidentally or only one surface; needs a dedicated/deeper spec.
- ❌ **Missing** — no spec; **coverage gap to close**.
- ⚠️ **Gap** — was covered, and is not any more. The claim is still true and still worth
  guarding; the spec that guarded it was retired for a reason recorded in the row. Distinct
  from ❌ because somebody decided this, and from ⬜ because the claim outlived its spec.
- ⬜ **Retired** — the claim itself no longer has a subject: the branch, build mode or
  screen it was about is gone. Nothing to re-cover.

Suite size: **353 spec files**, grouped audience → feature area under `tests/`:

| Folder | Specs | | Folder | Specs |
|---|---:|---|---|---:|
| `consumer/flatmates` | 52 | | `consumer/property` | 31 |
| `mobile` | 44 | | `consumer/list-property` | 25 |
| `admin` | 39 | | `consumer/account` | 23 |
| `consumer/search` | 28 | | `consumer/services` | 16 |
| `platform` (+`auth`, `help`) | 30 | | `consumer/society` | 12 |
| `consumer/dashboard` | 1 | | `ops` | 12 |
| | | | `consumer/home` | 8 |

Counts are re-derived from the tree, not maintained by hand — a hand-maintained figure here
has drifted every time it has been checked. The 32 loose specs at `tests/` and `tests/consumer/`
are cross-cutting and belong to no one area.

Spec citations below are paths under `tests/`, minus the `.spec.js`; a spec with an unambiguous
name may be cited by that name alone. `scripts/check-coverage-citations.mjs` fails if one of them
stops existing — a stale citation reads as coverage that is not there. It validates both forms,
but the bare form only inside the spec column, since a bare name is indistinguishable from prose
anywhere else. If you add a citation style it does not recognise, teach it the style rather than
relying on it to stay quiet.

Retry-camera is proven by counting `getUserMedia` calls (1 → 2), not by the error clearing. There is no OCR: Review shows photos only and the submit carries no `claims` field — the reviewer reads the number off the images. The server-chosen selfie pose is held to being load-bearing: the capture button starts disabled on one requested pose and is released only by the stall fallback, which says so on screen rather than claiming the check passed. The two exits from a decided case are also pinned: the button on a pending case must actually leave the flow, and a rejected retry must land on consent rather than the document picker. A 5xx on submit is pinned to the applicant's own words: the alert has to say the fault is ours and print the trace id, and must **not** repeat the server's last-resort "Something went wrong" — which, beside three photos just taken, reads as an accusation against the photos and sends the user to retake all three. Chromium, 390px viewport for capture; 10/10 passed. | **Aadhaar, PAN and mobile inputs show their printed format** (`IdInput`): Aadhaar groups as `1234 5678 9012` and stops at 12 digits, and Backspace over a group space removes the digit before it; PAN uppercases and drops any character out of place in `AAAAA9999A`; mobile shows `98765 43210` and drops a pasted `+91`; re-entering or pasting the same number over itself keeps every digit, since only a real delete takes the digit beside a group space. The shutter is located by `data-testid="capture-button"`, and must stay that way. Its label changes
per step ("Take the front" / "Take the back" / "Take the selfie") and the retake link beneath it
reads "Retake the front" — which *contains* "take the front", so a `getByRole` name regex matches
both controls and every assertion dies of a strict-mode violation rather than testing anything. The
2026-09-14 copy rewrite lost four tests to exactly that before the locators were anchored on the id.

The face model is the only WASM the app compiles in the browser; the CSP that permits it is guarded
by `frontend/scripts/check-csp.mjs`, because the header that ships to users is a different file from
the one the dev server serves.

Additional **ephemeral probes, not durable regression coverage**: native Chromium fake-media capture
reaches selfie for all three document types; PAN review/prepared images/retake and camera teardown
pass. Aadhaar and driving-licence require back capture and release streams on exit. WebKit desktop
QR and iPhone-emulated consent/denial UI pass, the latter with an injected denial API because this
Windows WebKit context has no native `mediaDevices`. Physical Android/iPhone remain untested.

**Open failures:** Retry camera does not request a new stream after denial. The five passing specs therefore do not establish
production-ready verification. Details and reproducible steps are in [the worklog](../tasks/todo.md).

## Viewport projects

The suite is **folder-routed** across viewport projects in *both* configs, because the
mobile-first overhaul made several behaviours viewport-dependent and a desktop-only run
would silently pass against a broken phone layout. A spec's folder is therefore a
functional choice, not just filing — it decides which viewports ever exercise it.

`tests/mobile/**` moved wholesale to the **live** suite in P5b wave 3, so the three
projects that matter now live in `playwright.config.js` — which, since the mock provider was
deleted, *is* the live suite:

| Project (default config) | Device | Runs |
|---|---|---|
| `chromium` | Desktop Chrome | every spec **except** `tests/mobile/` |
| `mobile` | Pixel 7 (412×915) | `tests/mobile/home-featured-first` and `home-flatmates-tile` + the converted cross-viewport specs |
| `mobile-small` | 360×640, `hasTouch` | all of `tests/mobile/` |

What is left of the old mock config is `playwright.nobackend.config.js`, and it keeps a single
`chromium` project. Its `mobile-small` project went in that same wave, because with the folder
gone it matched zero specs; its `mobile` project went the same way once the last cross-viewport
spec converted — and a project that matches nothing reports nothing.

`CROSS_VIEWPORT` is the explicit opt-in for specs that assert something genuinely
viewport-dependent. Adding a spec there is a
deliberate act — it doubles that spec's runtime, and a spec that only ever needed one
viewport should not be on the list. Converting a cross-viewport spec means **moving**
its entry to the live config's `mobile` project, never dropping it: a stale path matches
nothing and reports nothing, so halved coverage is silent. The live config's `mobile`
project takes the two width-sensitive `tests/mobile` specs and the converted cross-viewport list
(`referral-rewards`, `help/centre`, `help/help-urls`, `platform/i18n`). The mock list is now **empty** — every spec
that ever earned a second viewport has been converted.

`mobile-small` carries every `tests/mobile/` spec at the cramped width where bottom chrome,
tap targets and label wrapping break first; `mobile` repeats only the specs whose fold or
wrapping checks differ at 412×915.

**Two traps this layout has already caught, both worth remembering when writing a
cross-viewport spec:**

- *Collapsed chrome.* `Footer.jsx` renders each column as an accordion that starts
  **closed** below `sm`. A cross-viewport spec that clicks a footer link blind passes
  on desktop and fails on a phone against a footer that is behaving correctly — see
  `revealFooterLink()` in `platform/help/help-urls.spec.js`.
- *Unpainted tap targets.* Controls carrying `.tap-extend` are drawn smaller than 44px
  on purpose and restore the touch floor with a transparent 44px `::before`
  (`index.css`). `boundingBox()` measures the painted box, so asserting on it demands a
  44px *pill* and fails a compliant control. `mobile/tap-targets.spec.js` used to
  exempt `.tap-extend` wholesale; it now **unions the element box with its
  `::before`/`::after` geometry** instead, which is strictly stronger — deleting the
  pseudo-element from `index.css` fails the sweep, where the class name alone used to
  buy a pass. A single-element spec can still take the cheap route (see
  `mobile/home-taps.spec.js`), but never assert on `boundingBox()` alone.
- *Sweeps that measure a blank page.* Until D19x, `tap-targets.spec.js` swept at
  `networkidle`, which on this app fires ~350ms **before** React paints — six of seven
  routes were being measured with `innerText.length === 0` and passing vacuously. The
  sweep now gates on `appReady()` plus a `MIN_CANDIDATES` floor that is asserted again
  at measurement time. Any new sweep-style spec must do the same: a sweep that can
  silently measure an empty document is worse than no sweep.

| **Two controls over one column that contradict each other answer nothing, not everything.** Availability and Construction Status both narrow `possession`, so "Under Construction" plus "Ready to Move" is an empty intersection — and an empty intersection used to be sent as an empty array, which `buildQuery` omits, which the server reads as *not filtered*. The impossible filter returned the whole catalogue: the failure mode that looks most like success. `construction=` cannot carry the meaning either, because Spring binds an **absent** list param to an empty list too, so present-but-empty is indistinguishable from absent by the time `unmatchableIfAsked` sees it. The client now spells it with the reserved token `no.such.possession`, which survives sanitising, reaches `possession IN (…)` and can never match, `properties_possession_check` allowing three literals and NULL. The spec watches the wire for that token and only then asserts `Showing 0` — asserting the zero alone would pass against a server that had crashed | search-listings | consumer/search/contradictory-facets | ✅ |
| **An age or floor bound keeps the listings that never stated one, and says how many.** `cb.ge`/`cb.le` on a nullable column evaluates false against NULL, so a single nudge of either thumb silently deleted the silent majority — 21 of 33 approved buy rows state no age, 23 state no floor. The predicates now admit NULL, which would leave a filtered page reading as though every row had answered, so `unstatedFiltered` counts them out in the **same** scan as `totalElements` (one tuple query, not a second round trip) and `unstatedElements` rides back on the search envelope for the header to disclose. Deliberately not a coalesce: unstated is reported as unstated, never read as zero. The spec pins the **total**, not the first page — at page size 24 with 25 results, absence from page one proves nothing — and its third case is the vacuity guard: an unfiltered search shows no disclosure at all | search-listings | consumer/search/unstated-range-disclosure | ✅ |
| **Size and deposit are the two numbers a tenant shops on, and neither was filterable on rent.** Carpet area was gated in three separate places — the control lived in `BuyExtraSections`, the URL round-trip sat in the buy branch of `filtersToParams`/`paramsToFilters`, and `facetQuery` wrapped `minArea`/`maxArea` in `isBuy` — so relaxing any one alone yields a slider that paints and filters nothing; the spec asserts the **wire**, not just the count, because the seed can agree by accident. The deposit had no control on either side despite being the largest single sum an Indian tenant is asked for, routinely outweighing the rent difference the budget slider does let them express. `deposit` is nullable and 9 of the 26 approved rentals leave it blank, so the predicate admits NULL and `unstatedFiltered` counts them out, exactly as for age and floor: silence is not an offer of zero, and dropping those rows would answer "what can I afford" by hiding the listings whose deposit is merely unknown. Area is never null on a rental, so the same page must show **no** disclosure — that contrast proves the count is computed per question asked rather than emitted whenever a range is present. A hand-edited `?deposit=` on a sale link must not reach the wire | search-listings | consumer/search/rent-range-parity | ✅ |
| **Legacy posted-by URLs are ignored.** Draazy is owner-only, so a stale `?postedBy=agent` link must not paint a chip, must not survive the URL cleanup, and must not send `postedBy` to the public search API. The assertion lives in the facet spec because it exercises the same URL → filter state → wire path as the active filters | search-listings | consumer/search/new-facets | ✅ |
| **Flipping Rent ↔ Buy is a change of journey, not a new search.** `switchDeal` called `INITIAL(deal)`, discarding everything the two sides share — localities, societies, amenities, verification badges, the size band, the place-and-radius — so a tenant who priced up a Balewadi 2 BHK and then wondered what buying one costs had to rebuild the search from an empty panel. There were **two** resets and only the button was obvious: a separate effect reset again whenever `urlDeal` changed, so fixing the toggle alone would leave the back button and every in-app Rent/Buy link still clearing the search. Both paths now route through one `switchDealFilters`. What is *not* preserved carries equal weight: a monthly-rent band is not a sale budget, and tenant type, pet policy and move-in date have no sale equivalent, so carrying them would filter on a question nobody asked. Types and BHK are neither kept nor dropped but **coerced**, through the identical rules a cross-deal deep link already gets, so `3plus` on rent becomes `3` on buy. Land use gets its own case because it is the one shared key written *outside* the deal split in `filtersToParams`, so it is the one a reader is most likely to mistake for buy-only: plot and farmland are offered on both deals and an NA/residential zone means the same on a lease as on a sale. Runs at phone width because the toggle is `lg:hidden`; the URL path is exercised as a client-side navigation, since `goto` would re-read the whole state from the new URL and pass regardless. The last case pins the **opposite** rule, because carrying filters over is right for a bare deal change and wrong the moment the link narrows something itself: the footer's "Buy plots" must arrive clean, not quietly mean "buy plots in Balewadi with a gym" because that is where the tenant happened to be standing. `hasFilterParams` is what separates the two, and without it the preservation fix would have made every deal-naming deep link inherit the previous search | search-listings | consumer/search/deal-switch-preserves-filters | ✅ |
| **A filter the UI offers must have stock behind it.** Five controls were unfalsifiable rather than broken: the Farm Land chip and the Land Use filter had one agricultural row between them and none at all on rent, `rera_id` was null on every row so the verification chip and the +80 relevance weight it feeds were never exercised, and the Societies filter reached one building, where a single result cannot distinguish "narrowed correctly" from "returned the one row that exists". A green suite proved nothing about any of them. The seed now carries stock on both sides of each, and the spec asserts two things per filter — a non-empty result **and** a result smaller than the unfiltered catalogue, because a parameter the server silently ignores also returns more than none. | **Clearing the radius field narrows the search; it does not switch proximity off.** An emptied input stored `''`, which made `nearParams` drop the **centre point** along with the radius, so the whole filter went quiet while the chip kept naming the place on screen. The 25 km ceiling is one constant applied to the number input, the slider and any radius arriving from a shared URL; the commute axis has its own, because minutes and kilometres are equal at 25 by coincidence. | **A land listing is asked the three questions a Maharashtra buyer cannot proceed without, and asked them in its own units.** Every land-specific field was optional and the defaults were wrong: a farm opened on `sq.ft.` and was area-checked against a residential floor's 100–1,000,000 range, so a half-acre parcel was rejected as too small; the Zone picker offered four bare labels no Zone Certificate uses; and the two questions that decide a deal were booleans. `naSanctioned` as a checkbox could not say **deemed NA (s.42B / 42C)** at all — a different document and a different risk from an order in hand — and read as "still agricultural" to the detail page while reading as "unanswered" to everyone else; `satbara` asked whether a 7/12 exists, which every parcel has, instead of what its **Other Rights** column carries, which is the one place a mortgage, a Kul tenancy, a minor's entry or a boundary dispute is recorded. Both become three- and six-state answers, a farm for sale additionally states **who may lawfully buy it** (s.63 bars a non-agriculturist outright; s.63(1A) lets the Collector permit it), and all three are required in *both* wizards — each list offers an honest "not checked yet", so the requirement asks for a statement rather than for a title search, and `keepChangedErrors` exempts listings published before the rule. The unit now defaults per type and the area range follows the chosen unit, so the error quotes `0.1 and 100 Acre` rather than a square-foot figure the owner never entered. Retired values stay legal on the wire — the spec publishes a plot carrying `naSanctioned: true` and asserts the detail page renders it as "NA order sanctioned", which is what keeps a re-save from forcing an owner to relabel. Two disclosures ride along, neither of them a validation: the s.63 restrictions beside the eligibility answer on a sale, and the Kul-tenancy warning on a farm **for rent**, where a cultivator in continuous possession can become a protected tenant with a statutory right to buy. And s.194-IA's 1% TDS note is suppressed on agricultural land, which the section excludes by its own terms | list-property-wizard, property-detail | consumer/list-property/land-requirements, consumer/property/land-listing (`the buyer cost note quotes TDS on a … only when its land use attracts it`), `frontend/scripts/check-enum-vocabulary.mjs` | ✅ |

| **The owner-contact quota is the server's, not the browser's (D31b).** It used to be a `localStorage` counter, a locally-minted referral bonus and a gate that refused *before* making any request — three browser opinions, all reset by clearing site data and none of them visible from a second device. `GET /me/entitlements` now reports it and `POST /contacts/request` refuses with a 422 `contact_quota_exhausted`. The load-bearing assertions are the derivations: `used` is a count of `contact_requests` rows, so a repeat press on the same listing costs nothing and a refused press costs nothing either; and `referralBonus` (the referrer's +15 per accepted-contact referral) and `friendBonus` (the invited friend's +5, in the free bucket) are recomputed from the referrals that justify them, so a clawback or a refusal takes its contacts back with no grant to reverse. | **The "Add Property" picker's catalogue search runs only while its sheet is open.** The effect was ungated, so every visit to `/compare` fetched a 100-row page of listings for a dialog most visitors never open — and refetched it on each `ids` change, i.e. on every add and every remove. Asserted as a *transition* rather than a count — zero `\|/api/properties?\|` requests while the table is on screen, non-zero after the sheet opens — because the absolute count is a StrictMode artifact under the dev server, whereas "none, then some" is the actual claim | (search-listings) | consumer/search/compare ("the picker search runs only once the sheet is opened") | ✅ |

Asserted as a *transition*: the root's `overflowY` is checked to be un-locked with the table on screen and `hidden` once the sheet is open, so a page that never locked and a page that was already locked both fail. The lock is read off `document.documentElement`, not `body` — the root carries `overflow-x: clip`, which stops `body`'s overflow propagating to the viewport. The same omission on `DashboardReviewModal` was fixed alongside it | (search-listings) | consumer/search/compare ("the picker announces itself as a modal dialog while it holds the scroll lock") | ✅ |

| `/signin` `/signup` auth + OTP | auth | platform/auth/signin-otp-session, platform/auth/improvements, mobile/auth-keyboard | ✅ |
| **Sign In discloses nothing about whether a number is registered** — an unregistered mobile and a freshly registered one both stay on `/signin` and reveal the same OTP entry, with no "new here?" hint. The live API has deliberately no existence endpoint, because answering it publicly is a user-enumeration oracle; `Signin.jsx` gates the mock's convenience bounce to `/signup` behind `!authIsLive`, and that branch dies in P5c. Asserted as a pair on purpose — "the unknown number went to OTP" is only evidence of non-disclosure if a known number does the same | auth | platform/auth/signin-otp-session | ✅ |
| **A first-time account is asked for a name after the OTP, and only the first time.** Sign-in provisions an unknown mobile as a *nameless* buyer, so every account created this way stayed anonymous — owners fielded contact requests from a blank, and the dashboard nagged "Complete your profile" for something nobody had ever been asked. The obvious fix branches at the mobile step ("we don't recognise this number, tell us about yourself"), which is precisely the oracle the row above forbids: it needs a public answer to *does this number have an account?* before anyone has proved they own it. Moving the ask behind the code costs a genuine new user the same number of screens and gives away nothing, and it stops collecting details from people who mistype a number and never arrive. The trigger is **derived from the profile** (`name` blank), not from "this row was just inserted" — so somebody who abandons the step is asked again next time instead of staying nameless forever, and the concurrent-first-signin race that adopts an existing row cannot mis-fire it. Asserted as a pair for the same reason the disclosure row is: a new account being asked only evidences a new-account rule if a named one is *not*, so the spec signs the same person in twice, dropping the session in between. The name is read back through `apiLogin` rather than off the screen — a save that only updated React state would satisfy any assertion made against that tab. `liveAuth.signIn` completes the step for the whole suite (it races the field against the redirect, because a seeded fixture already has a name and would otherwise pay the full timeout on every sign-in) | auth | platform/auth/signin-otp-session | ✅ |
| **An account created seconds ago lands on the listings, not on its own dashboard.** The hub derives every card from real activity, so a just-provisioned account sees only zeros at its moment of highest intent. Both entry points make the decision from the profile returned *before* the profile patch: Sign In sees the blank name directly; Sign Up receives `wasNew` from its provider because it is login plus a patch, not a true create endpoint. The browser spec exercises both fallback branches: a new account lands on `/listings`; the same named account through Sign Up lands on `/dashboard`. An explicit `?next=` still outranks either fallback. No KYC prompt was added: the platform guardrail forbids a KYC nudge before a value moment. | auth | platform/auth/signin-otp-session, platform/auth/improvements | ✅ |
| **A `next=` cannot steer a freshly-authenticated session off-site or back to auth.** The shared guard rejects protocol-relative paths, backslashes (a slash to the URL parser), C0 controls stripped during resolution, malformed escapes, and any encoded path that resolves to `/signin`, `/signup`, or `/staff-login` — including `/%252e%252e%2fsignin`. Assertions require the expected fallback landing rather than merely the localhost origin, because `replaceState` throws for cross-origin URLs and a vulnerable guard would otherwise leave the browser on the sign-in page. `/staff-login` is separately exercised because it additionally role-filters internal destinations. | auth | platform/auth/improvements ("a hostile `next` cannot steer a freshly-authenticated session off-site") | ✅ |
| **The refresh token is unreadable by scripts, and the session renews anyway.** It used to sit in `localStorage` beside the access token, so any XSS payload took a thirty-day credential; it is now an `HttpOnly` cookie (`__Host-draazy_rt` in production, `draazy_rt` over plain-HTTP dev where a browser rejects the prefix; `Path=/`) which a payload can still *spend* from our own origin but can no longer *exfiltrate*, so the stolen capability dies with the page instead of outliving it by a month. Both halves are asserted because either alone is worthless — hiding the token is trivial if you break renewal, and renewing is trivial if you leave it readable. The absence is checked in the two places a script can actually look (`localStorage`, `document.cookie`) and the presence through the browser's own jar, since without that third check the two absences read equally as "no token was ever issued", i.e. as the feature being broken rather than secured. Renewal is then forced by **tampering** the access token so the next call 401s: waiting out fifteen minutes is not a test, and deleting it exercises a different branch (`http.js` treats an absent access token as signed-out and never refreshes). This is the only level at which any of it can be tested — MockMvc has no cookie jar, no `document.cookie` and no same-origin policy, so a backend test can prove the attribute was *set* but not that a browser honours it or that `credentials: 'include'` sends it back | auth | platform/auth/signin-otp-session | ✅ |
| **"Remember this device for 30 days" survives a browser that wipes web storage.** Safari's ITP deletes script-writable storage — `localStorage`, IndexedDB, `document.cookie` writes — after seven days without first-party interaction, but leaves server-set cookies alone. So on day eight a remembered user held a cached-profile-less, access-token-less browser and a refresh cookie with three weeks left, and nothing ever spent it: an absent access token reads as "signed out" everywhere else in the client, deliberately, because for an ordinary request that is what it means. Thirty days quietly meant seven, plus a stranded credential. The fix is a second, deliberately **readable** cookie (`__Host-draazy_session` in production, `draazy_session` over plain-HTTP dev where a browser rejects the prefix; `Path=/`, no identity in it) set beside the refresh token, which — being server-set — ITP spares; the cold boot refreshes exactly when it is present, so the anonymous majority still costs zero requests. Reproduced by clearing web storage while leaving the jar intact, which is precisely the state ITP leaves behind: Playwright cannot run a seven-day timer, but the input to the code under test is reproducible exactly. The jar is asserted non-empty first, or the whole test would pass on a session that was simply destroyed. The marker also carries *whether* the session was meant to last (`1` vs `0`), because `remember` has to be restated on every rotation and the client used to derive it from which storage tier held the tokens — the exact derivation this wipe destroys. Without that, the refresh that rescues the session reports "not remembered" and trades the 30-day cookie for a session one: a silent demotion strictly worse than the bug being fixed, so the recovered session's tier and cookie expiry are both asserted. The marker is now consulted *first* and the tier only as a fallback, because the tier records where a write landed rather than what the user chose, and one unwritable-`localStorage` moment would otherwise trigger the same demotion with no wipe involved at all. Browser-only by nature — MockMvc has neither a cookie jar nor a `localStorage` to be robbed of | auth | platform/auth/signin-otp-session | ✅ |
| **The same rescue does not promote a session the user declined to have remembered.** The pair to the row above, and it only means anything as a pair: `remember` is restated from a single value on every rotation, so a test that asserts only the remembered case stays green against a client that hardcodes `true`, and one that asserts only this case stays green against a client that hardcodes `false`. Together they pin the flag to what the user actually chose. Signs in with the checkbox **un**checked and asserts the marker reads `0` with a session-lifetime cookie *before* the wipe — so a failure reads as "login recorded the wrong thing" rather than as a broken recovery — then clears both storages, reloads, and asserts the rescued session landed in `sessionStorage` with `localStorage` still empty and the rotated refresh cookie still session-scoped. Honestly, the wipe here is not Safari's seven-day eviction: an unremembered session dies with the browser anyway, so ITP never gets the chance. It is any mid-session storage loss with the jar intact — a private-mode quota error, a cleaner extension, a crash — which lands in the same recovery path reading the same marker, and that path is what is under test | auth | platform/auth/signin-otp-session | ✅ |
| **Signing out leaves nothing that claims a session.** The counterpart to the marker above, and the reason it is a cookie the *server* clears rather than a flag the client sets: a hint outliving its revoked token would send every later cold boot into a refresh that can only 401 — a clean logout turned into a request shaped exactly like reuse-detection tripping, on the one path where that pattern is watched for. Both cookies are asserted gone from the context jar, since a "clear" built with a mismatched path silently leaves the original in place | auth | platform/auth/signin-otp-session | ✅ |
| **Signing out ends the access token at once**, not when it expires; suspending or archiving an account does the same (the JWT filter refuses a token issued before `users.sessions_revoked_at`) | auth | platform/auth/signin-otp-session ("signing out ends the access token at once") | ✅ |
| **A sign-out the server never hears about still ends the session on this device.** `POST /auth/logout` is best-effort by design — a user on a dying connection must not be trapped in a signed-in app — but the marker cookie made that leniency dangerous once the cold boot began acting on it: the local state was cleared, the marker and the unrevoked refresh cookie survived, and the *next* launch used them to sign the user straight back in. On a shared or borrowed device that is the previous person's account reappearing after they visibly signed out. The client therefore expires the marker itself, so the recovery path is shut even when the revocation is not. Forced by aborting the logout request at the network layer, which is the only honest way to produce the state; the assertion is a fresh navigation, because anything short of a reload is testing the in-memory context rather than the boot decision | auth | platform/auth/signin-otp-session | ✅ |
| **A wrong OTP says how many guesses are left, and the last one blocks the form.** The per-code guess ceiling is `draazy.otp.max-verify-attempts` (default 3, env `DRAAZY_OTP_MAX_VERIFY_ATTEMPTS`, refused outside 1..20 at boot), and the login `401` carries `attemptsRemaining` so the form can say what is left rather than repeating one flat "that didn't work" until a `429` arrives looking like a server fault. The number rides in the JSON envelope rather than a response header because the API exposes no CORS headers at all, so a browser on another origin could never read one. Asserted as exact numbers — 2 left, then 1, then the dead end — since an off-by-one either burns a guess the user was owed or offers one the server will refuse, and both read as a working form; the count is derived after the attempt is recorded, which is where that error would live. The message is read as *rendered*, not as the server's prose: the API speaks English and this form ships in three languages, so a screen showing the server's sentence would itself be the bug. The last test is the one worth having — typing another digit must **not** dismiss the blocker, because only a fresh code can help and clearing it on a keystroke walks the user straight back into a refusal. Only a code the server can no longer accept blocks the form: a `429` is deliberately not read as one unless it carries `otp_attempts_exhausted`, because `POST /auth/login` also passes the platform's per-IP write limiter and answers that with the generic `rate_limited`, so on shared mobile egress a stranger's traffic would otherwise strand a user holding a valid code behind a permanently disabled button | auth | platform/auth/signin-otp-session | ✅ |
| **Sign Up answers the same refusals in the same words.** A separate screen posting to the same `POST /auth/login`, so it spends the same guess budget and meets the same dead ends — and its catch has to sort them into two piles that look identical at the call site. A `422` names the field to fix and keeps the server's English, because a generic line would throw away the only useful part of it; everything else is about the code or the account and must be translated. Splitting on the *presence of a count* rather than on the status gets that backwards for the two refusals with no count to carry — an expired code, which the 5-minute TTL makes the common case on a form that also collects a name and an email, and `signups_closed`, which this is the only screen that can raise. Both then render an English sentence to a Marathi user, which is the exact bug the countdown exists to remove, and neither is visible on a happy path. Asserted with the same three numbers and the same keystroke guard as the Sign In row through `getByRole('alert')`, so the result also proves the verification feedback is announced | auth | platform/auth/signin-otp-session | ✅ |
| **Staff Login shares the OTP attempt cap without silently spending a colleague's code.** The internal console verifies through the same `POST /auth/login` route, so it can neither invent a different budget nor leave its Verify control live after the third wrong code. Its English-only copy retains the server detail but adds the exact remaining number, in an always-mounted alert region, and the final allowed wrong guess says to request a new code before disabling verification. The mobile field is also locked while send or verification is in flight, so the page cannot label a late response for one number as a code sent to another. This is separate from the consumer screen test because shared API does not make two independently implemented UI state machines the same code | auth | platform/auth/signin-otp-session | ✅ |
| **A refused *send* reaches the user, and a failed resend outranks the blocker it replaces.** Every one of these screens had two `role="alert"` regions — one for the send step, one for verification — and the send one lived inside the `otpSent` branch, so a refusal on the very first tap had nowhere to render: the button returned to "Send OTP" and nothing said why. There is now a single region mounted for the form's whole lifetime, `sr-only` when empty so it stays in the accessibility tree, and its precedence is asserted at the one point where the two messages compete: a spent code leaves a terminal blocker on screen, and the resend that is the only way out of it fails. If the retained blocker wins, the page repeats "request a new code" over a request that just failed, which is advice the user has already taken | auth | platform/auth/signin-otp-session | ✅ |
| **Markup a user types renders as text on every surface that echoes it.** A support ticket whose subject and body carry an inert canary (`<b data-canary>`) is read back by the customer on `/support` and by the desk on `/ops/support`; a `?q=` canary is echoed on `/listings`, `/societies` and `/help/search`. Each asserts the literal text is visible and that no `[data-canary]` element exists, so any `dangerouslySetInnerHTML`-style regression on these views fails. The backend round trip of the same strings is `HostileTextRoundTripTest` | security | platform/security/output-encoding | ✅ |
| **A tampered browser session grants nothing.** A buyer who rewrites the cached `draazyUser.role` to `admin` is bounced off `/admin`, the cache is revalidated back to `buyer`, and no `/api/admin` call answers 2xx — the role is the server's, never the cache's. A corrupt cached user with a garbage token lands on `/signin` without a page error | security | platform/security/session-state | ✅ |
| **A refused *send* is announced in the app's own words, never the thrower's.** The send catch rendered `err.message` verbatim, so whatever object reached it wrote the sentence: a backend refusal in English on a trilingual form, or — the way this was found — the services seam's own developer diagnostic, `[services] could not load the "auth" provider.`, printed on a real phone's sign-in screen. A phone holding a service-worker-cached shell from the previous deploy boots fine and fails only when it first reaches a lazy chunk, which on that shell is the auth provider; no request is ever made, so nothing server-side can see it. `classifyOtpSendError` now maps the failure to a key the same way `classifyOtpVerifyError` does. The seam tags it `provider_load_failed` — what it observes, not a cause it cannot establish, since a dropped radio rejects a dynamic import identically to a chunk a redeploy removed — and each classifier consults `navigator.onLine` before choosing between "reload, we updated" and the offline line, because telling someone with no signal to reload is advice that cannot work. Every key is in an eager namespace, because `OwnerConsentModal` and `StaffLogin` also carry this hook and declare no lazy namespace. Asserted by routing `POST /auth/login` to a 429 carrying its own English prose and requiring the alert to hold the translated line *and not contain* the server's, on both the first send and the resend — the two places the old code leaked it | auth | platform/auth/signin-otp-session | ✅ |
| **Every sign-in refusal names the next step, and a rate limit names the wait.** A 429 reads "Too many tries. Wait 47 seconds and try again." from the envelope's `retryAfterSeconds` (minutes past 60 s; "a minute" when absent) instead of "a few seconds" against an hour-long budget. A staff mobile on `/signin` is told to use Staff sign-in, a send-side 5xx says the code could not be sent, and Signup's 409/422 after the profile write say what to fix instead of printing "Request validation failed", and the retry patches the profile rather than re-spending the OTP; a verified account's name is never re-sent. `/staff-login` and `/staff-invite` go through `lib/staffAuthError.js` and never print `err.message` for 401/400/422/429/5xx/transport failures — no "Invalid credentials", "HTTP 502" or the seam's `[services]` diagnostic; 403/409 keep the server's sentence (suspended, not activated, sign-in off, authenticator already set) only when it carries our `error` code, so a proxy's 403 page is never shown. A suspension is 403 `account_suspended` ("This account is suspended…"), distinct from the send-step bot-check 403 ("Reload the page…"); the owner-consent modal counts attempts down like sign-in instead of always saying "did not match" | auth | platform/auth/signin-otp-session ("an OTP delivery refusal…"), platform/auth/improvements ("sign-up with a taken email…"), platform/auth/signin-otp-session ("a suspended account is told so…"), consumer/flatmates/owner-consent ("a wrong owner code…"), admin/staff-two-factor ("a wrong password is refused…", "the invite page refuses a bad code in plain words", "staff sign-in refusals say what to do next") | ✅ |
| **A shell that can no longer load its own code reloads itself, once, instead of telling the user to.** The row above stopped the leak and left the advice: "Draazy was updated — reload the page", handed to someone mid-sign-in on a phone. Nothing about that is a choice. A rejected dynamic import is memoised by the host against the specifier, so the domain is dead for the life of the document and every further tap fails identically; the page holds nothing that could still be submitted, so a reload costs the user nothing they had not already lost. `healStaleShell` therefore performs it, from the four catches where someone is visibly stuck — the OTP send, the three verify screens, and the identity submit — and deliberately **not** from the seam itself, where a background load failing would take a half-filled listing wizard with it. It asks the service-worker registration to update *before* navigating, because a bare reload is answered out of the very precache naming the missing chunk and the new document would be exactly as stale as the old one (`skipWaiting` + `clientsClaim` hand over as soon as the replacement installs). A `sessionStorage` flag bounds it to once per tab: a second identical failure disproves the stale-shell guess, and without the guard the tab would cycle forever on a chunk that is simply gone. Asserted by aborting the `authProvider.js` request — how a removed hashed chunk really fails — then reading the reload off an emptied form, and re-submitting to prove the second failure stops reloading and speaks | auth | platform/auth/signin-otp-session | ✅ |
| **The resend countdown is the server's number, not the browser's.** `useOtpFlow` counted down a hardcoded 30s while `OtpSendBudget` refuses a second code for 60 in a deployment, so the button came back halfway through the gap and the person who waited for it and pressed it got a rate-limit error instead of a code. No constant could have been right: local and e2e run a mock sender and enforce no gap at all, which is also why this cannot be caught by a suite that only runs against those profiles — the two environments where the timer is honest are the two the tests use. The send acknowledgement now carries `resendAfterSeconds` (the configured cooldown, `OtpResendCooldownContractTest` proving it is read from configuration and not another literal) and the hook counts exactly that down, treating `0` as a real answer rather than a missing one. | **Crossing that line costs the visitor nothing, including the keystroke they were mid-way through.** The draft autosave is debounced and its cleanup clears the pending timer on unmount, so navigating to `/signin` destroyed the very write the redirect was relying on — the last field typed simply vanished, which is the one loss a user would notice and the hardest to reproduce deliberately. `useFormDraft` grew a `flush()` the gate calls before it navigates. **The proof rests on `page.clock`, and it has to.** The first version of this test simply clicked without an intervening wait, on the theory that it would therefore land inside the debounce window — and it passed with `flush()` deleted, because every `fill` and every locator resolution is a CDP round trip and the gap between the last keystroke and the click is not reliably under 400ms. It was a green test asserting nothing. `clock.install({ time })` alone does not help either; it seeds a fake clock but leaves it running, which a scratch probe proved by watching the draft get written after three real seconds. Only `clock.pauseAt()` stops `setTimeout` firing, which makes the debounce window unbounded and the flush the *sole* way the draft can reach disk — mutation-verified red (`Received string: ""`) with the flush removed. The clock is then **resumed** before the sign-in half, because a paused clock starves a fresh page load of every boot timer and the wizard never paints, which reads as the restore losing the answers. With that in place the row proves the round trip end to end: the redirect carries `reason=services` and a `next` back to the wizard, the sign-up link carries it too, the draft holds the un-debounced answers, and after signing in all four property fields repopulate and `Next` returns | rent-agreement / auth | consumer/services/rent-agreement | ✅ |

Four tests here spent that period running as anonymous visitors while claiming to be a buyer, and none failed, because none asserted anything an account was needed for; the gate above is the first thing in the file to read `isIn`, which is what surfaced it. | **Filed as a valuation, deliberately.** Rent-agreement is the one priced desk, so it opens at `awaiting-payment`, and `ServiceRequestStatus.ALLOWED` lets that reach only `new` or `cancelled` — `STAFF_SETTABLE` excludes `new` precisely so a status endpoint cannot skip the till, and only the signed webhook moves it. Before this it read "Pending". **Tapping the chip explains it in place, using the same `Tip` popover the property page uses for its scores, and the card's link does not fire.** For the host, the tip says the badge is waiting on the property owner's OTP consent or gives the moderator's reason on a rejection. | A 409 from the one-open-unpaid-order cap is reported as itself. Branched on the status rather than `err.message`: the code is the generic `conflict`, and the message is English-only while the page is not | plans-billing-refer | consumer/services/plans-checkout ("a second checkout while an order is still unpaid names that order, instead of inviting a retry that cannot work") | ✅ |
| A payment that clears while the pending screen is open becomes the success screen: checkout polls `GET /me/entitlements` every 3s (up to 30s, then a "Check again" button) and shows "Payment successful!" only once the pack or pass is reflected there. | The one-open-unpaid-order cap (D160) holds at the server for both families that carry it: a second subscription order while one is pending is 409 naming the order; two simultaneous subscription orders or two simultaneous requests for the same priced desk open exactly one (201 + 409); the cap is per user, so another account is unaffected | billing-referrals | consumer/services/payment-open-order-cap | ✅ |

| **`referralRewards` is a server document, and it withdraws the routes rather than the entitlement.** The retired mock twin read `draazyDB_v5` out of `localStorage` for both halves — the flag value and the arithmetic it gated — so the fake stored the client's own vocabulary and handed it back, and seventeen tests agreed with themselves. Live the flag round-trips `PUT /admin/settings` → public `GET /bootstrap` (`flags`), which is the route the consumer screens read, and the assertions are that turning it off drops the refer CTA from the exhausted upsell and the seeker track and its earnings from `/refer` while leaving the paid route and the base rent-agreement programme standing. Since the referral redesign the flag is also enforced server-side: with it off `POST /referrals/redeem` refuses with the same 409 as every other refusal and qualification stops, while a bonus already earned still counts (`referral-rewards`, `referral-redesign`). | plans-billing-refer | consumer/services/referral-rewards (chromium + mobile) | ✅ |
| **A friend who joins from a link is paid in contacts, and the referrer is paid when an owner accepts the friend.** Signup carries a visible optional "Referral code" field prefilled from `?ref` (or the first-touch code kept 30 days in `localStorage`), redeems it after OTP, and sends the device id from `dz_did`. The seeker track qualifies on the friend's first contact request that a *third-party* owner accepts on an approved, live listing (the referrer approving it is refused as `self_dealing`): the referrer gets +15 (`referralBonus`), the friend +5 in the free bucket (`friendBonus`, also on `/refer` as "+5 free contacts"). The assertions are the two balances on `GET /me/entitlements` and the `refer-*` testids reading them, not the toast. A refused code says only "That code couldn't be applied" ~ no reason, by design | plans-billing-refer | consumer/services/referral-redesign (`a friend signing up from a link...`, `a code from the first visit survives...`) | ✅ |
| **The owner track pays a free agreement and a Featured window, and takes them back with the verification.** The friend's first ownership verification (the admin grant, not a SQL stamp) gives the referrer `agreements.free` 1 (`refer-agreements` reads "0 / 1") and Features the friend's listing for `referralOwnerFeaturedDays` (14, `properties.featured_days`) ~ featured on day 10, not on day 15. Revoking the verification inside the 30-day window claws it back (`verification_revoked`) | plans-billing-refer, property-verification | consumer/services/referral-redesign (`the owner track pays...`, `withdrawing the verification...`) | ✅ |
| **Every refused redeem looks the same.** An account older than the signup window, one that already has a listing or a contact request, a code used from the referrer's own `X-Device-Id`, and a switched-off programme all get the identical 409 `{error,message}` ~ so a probe learns nothing about which rule fired ~ while a different device is accepted | plans-billing-refer | consumer/services/referral-redesign (`a code is refused for an old account...`), consumer/services/referral-rewards (`the switch is the server's too...`) | ✅ |
| **Caps and clawbacks are automatic.** A referrer already at `referralQualifyPerMonth` gets the next qualification rejected `cap_reached` and the friend's `friendBonus` falls to 0; suspending the referee inside `referralClawbackDays` takes the reward back (`clawed-back`, `referee_suspended`). A referrer who approves their own referee's contact request earns nothing (`rejected`, `self_dealing`), one owner approving a second friend of the same referrer is a ring (`high_risk`, only the first is paid), and a redeem with no `X-Device-Id` is never scored low. Rows are filled and aged with SQL, since neither needs a browser | referrals-fraud | consumer/services/referral-redesign (`a referrer at the monthly cap...`, `...self_dealing`, `...ring...`, `a redeem with no device id...`, `suspending the referee...`) | ✅ |

Save is proved by reading the new value back **from the API** rather than off the console the write just re-rendered, with the status and the public route asserted after it so "stayed live" can be told apart from "was never live". | **The Duplicates desk is answered by the server, and the tab that shipped for three days answered from `localStorage`.** D249 measured it: 71 listings on the server, four titles repeating, and the KPI painted a confident **`0`** — because `findDuplicateClusters()` ran a union-find over the seeded `draazyDB_v5` fixture, and `resolveDuplicate` "archived" the loser into the browser, where no operator and no auditor would ever find it. The tab was gated out of live builds; D255 rebuilt it properly rather than leaving the gate, deriving clusters on demand from the same three signals the write-time `ListingDuplicateProbe` uses (address key, electricity meter, photo hash) and persisting a dismissal in `listing_duplicate_dismissals` keyed by a **sha-256 of the sorted member ids** — so "not a duplicate" settles that exact set and a third colliding listing correctly asks again, a rule that falls out of the key rather than being enforced anywhere. Two findings came out of writing the spec, neither of which the mock suite could have produced. **(1)** The prototype's merge wrote `duplicateFlag` and `duplicateOf` — two columns no table on this platform has ever had; the live merge archives the losers and deliberately leaves the keeper untouched, which is asserted. **(2)** An owner *cannot* self-post a duplicate: `POST /me/listings` answers `422 listing_quota_exhausted` at one live listing on the free tier, so the same-owner case arrives almost entirely through the quota-exempt concierge route — which is both why `sameOwner` exists (the cross-owner probe cannot see it by construction) and why that test posts through `POST /admin/properties`. Every fixture is created **over the wire**, because seeded rows are raw SQL and bypass `duplicates.reindex(p)`: measured, all 123 seeded properties carried a null `address_key`, so a spec built on the seed would have proved nothing while passing. Mutation-proven: constant-folding the cluster signature and pinning `sameOwner` to `false` turned exactly the three predicted tests red. The mock `duplicates.spec.js` is deleted \u2014 its own header said it became convertible the day this endpoint existed | properties | admin/duplicates | \u2705 |

| **A host can see and withdraw their own rooms (`GET /me/flatmate-rooms`, `DELETE /flatmates/rooms/{id}`).** The dashboard panel read the browser store, so a host who posted a room from their phone could not see it on their laptop, and "Delete" removed a local row while the room stayed on the public board. The host read is the interesting half because it is deliberately *not* the public one: it includes `pending` and `removed` (a host must be able to see that their post is stuck in review — hiding it is how a moderation queue becomes a black hole), excludes archived, and populates `ownerMobile` with the caller's own number, which the public board masks. It is asserted as caller-scoped rather than filtered — a widening query param on it must narrow to the caller anyway, because an unknown param is silently ignored rather than rejected, so "I passed a filter and got rows back" proves nothing on its own. Withdrawal is pinned on its three refusals as much as its success: 404 on a second withdrawal (so a retry cannot report a phantom success), 403 for a stranger, and 409 for a `split_room` that another party is depending on. | **A commercial listing publishes the owner's own answers instead of a fabricated fit-out.** Every commercial-only answer — `shellType`, `washrooms`, `camCharges`, `suitableFor`, `fixtures`, `powerBackup`, `pantry` — was written into `form_details` and then never read back: `PropertyResponse` promoted five residential keys and `toViewModel` mapped none of the rest, so `RentDetails.jsx` fell through to a tier-keyed `COMMERCIAL_INVENTORY` and printed "Warm Shell · Power Backup · Washrooms" on *every* commercial rental, as a claim the owner had made, regardless of what they entered. The fallback is deleted rather than corrected — a fit-out list with no answers behind it is worse than an empty one — and the keys now cross the wire in a nested `Commercial` object, so a residential response never carries the concept at all. `lockIn`/`noticePeriod` are promoted the same way (the tiles read "None / 1 month" on every listing because the fallback was `?? 1`, and "None" is a term the owner agreed to while silence is not), and the **Suitable For** tile reads `suitableFor` rather than `tenants`, which had an office advertising "Family / Bachelors". It now returns `null` until a sub-type exists and every profile-scoped block is gated on it; `suitableForFor` partitions the server's existing eight-value enum by profile rather than inventing labels, because `ListingFormDetails.ARRAY_ENUMS` answers 422 to anything new. Nothing used to reset on a sub-type change — `changePropertyType` fired only on the property type — so Office → Server/UPS Room + a Lift amenity → Warehouse published both, in a form that could no longer show them, let alone unpick them; `changeCommercialType` clears fixtures, `suitableFor`, amenities, `pantry` and the profile-scoped specs and re-tags staged photos to `Other`, the one category all three profiles offer. `age` joins `TYPE_SPECIFIC_KEYS` (commercial never asks for it, so a residential answer left behind published unseen) and is dropped from the commercial branch outright — `shellType` is the fit-out date a tenant is actually pricing. | **A furniture / amenity tile is a control, not a `<div>` that happens to have an `onClick`.** `FeatureSelector` renders the one grid on the wizard a user taps a dozen times, and it carried no role, no `tabIndex` and no pressed state — so a keyboard user could not reach a single tile, a screen reader was told neither that it was operable nor whether it was chosen, and the app-wide press response (`@media (hover: none)`, which selects on `button, [role="button"], [role="switch"], …` and never on tag names) skipped it, leaving it the only grid on the form that stayed visually inert under a finger. All three states pass a click-based test, which is why the assertion is a **key press**: focus a predefined tile, press Space, and demand `aria-pressed` flip with the `checked` class, then Enter to flip it back. The Space handler calls `preventDefault`, so the spec pins `window.scrollY` against a baseline taken *after* `focus()` — focusing scrolls the tile into view, and an assertion against `0` fails a correct implementation. Custom entries are asserted separately because they are a different control wearing the same class: activating one *removes* it, so it is named `Remove <label>` rather than carrying a pressed state that would read to a screen reader as "this amenity is off". | **A plot or a farm can be posted with the least it is genuinely able to state.** The land wizard was already the shortest in the product, so what was left was not length. The address step refused to advance without a **project / layout name** whenever the stored line could not be decomposed — a question a farm on a gat number cannot answer at all, and one a plot resold out of a 1990s scheme usually cannot either; commercial had already received exactly this relief via `isIndustrial`, and land now shares it, so the unit/project pair is demanded only where a unit and a project exist. Three smaller frictions ride along, all of them the same mistake of asking a built property's question of raw land: a farm was asked its **length × width in feet** beside an area it gave in guntha or acre (the pair implies a rectangle an irregular parcel does not have — approach road stays, since width of access prices both types), the strength meter scored a **MahaRERA number** on an open-plot sale where the number is optional and MahaRERA registers layouts rather than the individual NA plot an owner is reselling, and it scored the layout name it no longer asks for. The load-bearing negative is the type switch: `construction` was not in `TYPE_SPECIFIC_KEYS`, so a flat's **possession** answer survived into a plot and published a claim the land form renders no control for — which is the leak `isPreCompletion`'s land exclusion existed to survive rather than to fix. Driven through the wizard on purpose: the claim is about what the form lets an owner submit, which an API post cannot prove — the wire shape of a published parcel stays with `consumer/property/land-listing`. Land area is also named per type in `toEditForm` rather than recovered by `vm.carpetArea ?? vm.area`, so the round-trip no longer rests on a fallback that exists for legacy rows | list-property-wizard | consumer/list-property/land-minimum (`a buy Open Plot publishes without naming a project or layout` and its three siblings, `switching a flat to a plot posts neither its possession nor its age`, `land listings: a farm is asked no length and width, a farm sale to rent drops buyer eligibility, and the meter does not dock a land sale for no MahaRERA number`) | ✅ |

| **No property photo is refused for its size; compressing it is the product's job.** `image.worker.js` made one bounded attempt — a 2,560px frame at quality 0.95 with `alwaysKeepResolution`, retried once to a 0.9025 floor — and `prepareUpload.js` turned anything still over 1 MB into "This file cannot fit under 1 MB at the supported quality. Please choose a smaller original." An owner photographing their own flat on a modern phone has no smaller original and no way to make one, so the wizard refused the only evidence the listing needed, and the failure scaled with the quality of the camera. The refusal is gone for photos: quality is spent first down a six-rung ladder (0.92 → 0.35) so an ordinary shot keeps every pixel, and only a frame that still will not fit sheds resolution — stepped by the ratio the last round *measured* rather than a fixed guess, since JPEG bytes track pixel count, down to a 320px floor that is tens of kilobytes at the lowest rung. That makes fitting a property of the loop rather than a hope, which is why `browser-image-compression` could go with it: its own iteration rules were the thing that had to be reasoned around, and the native `convertToBlob` ladder also drops the intermediate PNG re-encode and releases the decoded bitmap before the first encode instead of after the last. **The same worker serves documents, and there the refusal is kept on purpose** — a scan ladders only to 0.62 and 1,600px, because a deed degraded past reading is worse for the moderator who has to check it than one the owner is asked to retake, and an illegible file is indistinguishable from a forged one. The spec asserts the invariant on two hostile full-RGB-noise originals, one under the 2,560px cap and one at it, and pins the trade-off **conditionally on measured bytes**: the frame stays whole exactly when its own quality-0.35 encode fits, and must be smaller when it does not — with `[true, false]` asserted on that predicate across the pair, so neither a version that always downscales nor one that never does can pass, and a future encoder improvement cannot collapse both shots onto one branch unnoticed. Each original is put through a second time as a document, which must come back at 1,600px or better or not at all. The 25 MB and 48 MP guards stay (they bound decode memory before any pixels are allocated, not output quality) and keep their own rejection tests, but no longer appear in the picker's guidance: a limit quoted upfront deters the 99% who will never reach it, so only the file that actually hits one is told | list-property-wizard, documents | upload-policy (`never refuses a photo for its size: quality is spent before resolution`, `compresses an oversized image without spending resolution it does not have to`, `rejects disallowed formats, disguised files and excessive inputs`) | ✅ |

| The listing editor reads the owner's listing from the server, **and in the shape the wizard uses** (D237) — `?edit=<id>` used to prefill from `getListing(id)` in localStorage, so an owner opening it on a second device, after clearing site data, or from the enquiry panel's "Add photos" link (built from a server id this browser never held) got the blank default form — and submitting it would have PATCHed those defaults over a listing that was not blank. It reads `propertyService.myListing` → `GET /me/listings/{id}` now, owner-scoped because a listing under moderation is not on the public endpoint at all. Repointing the read was only half of it: a mock record carries its own `form` snapshot while a server row carries the contract's names (`type` for `propertyType`, `desc` for `description`, `3` for `"3 BHK"`), so the first version filled almost nothing and every mock spec stayed green — hence `toEditForm`, whose field set is pinned to `ListingUpdate` rather than to the visible form, because `toListingUpdate` drops only `undefined` and a field the prefill cannot restore is a field the next save erases. The live test asserts values, not a heading: carpet area (free text, so no default can fake it) and BHK (which has a default, and was seeded away from it). Three consequences are asserted alongside: the duplicate guard's CTA opens `?edit=` on the id it holds instead of detouring to `/dashboard`; an edit whose listing failed to load refuses to save rather than saving the defaults; and an address the owner never retyped is not recomposed from empty parts | list-property-wizard | property-integration (`the edit form prefills from the server, not from this browser (D237)`), consumer/property/dup-modal (`Re-listing the same unit shows the duplicate guard modal and blocks the post`), consumer/list-property/edit-policy | ✅ |
| **An owner can take their own listing down, and the slot comes back** (D238) — the dashboard's Delete ran `setListingStatus(id, 'deleted')`: the moderator's route, which an owner is not authorised for, carrying a status that route rejects anyway — and it was not awaited, so both failures were swallowed, the owner was toasted "deleted", and `refreshListings()` put the listing straight back. With the quota enforced server-side (D235) that was not untidiness, it was a ceiling with no exit: the free tier is one listing at a time and this was the only way to let go of one. Now `propertyService.takeListingDown` → `DELETE /me/listings/{id}`, which existed and had never been called, kept separate from `archiveListing` because that is the staff path and takes a reason an owner does not owe anyone. Relabelled "Take down" since the server soft-archives — "Delete" promised an erasure the product does not perform. The live test asserts the whole loop: a second post is refused 422 while the first stands, the DELETE answers 200, the row comes back from `/me/listings` marked `archived` (the API keeps the owner's whole file; the dashboard is what filters it), and the retried post is accepted 201 | list-property-wizard | property-integration (`an owner can take their own listing down, and it frees a quota slot (D238)`) | ✅ |

| **Owner-private lead notes live on the server, not in the browser.** A note and a follow-up date are typed into the lead sheet, and the load-bearing assertions read them back from somewhere the writing browser does not own — the API directly, then a **second browser context** signed in as the same owner. Both are necessary: a reload-and-still-there check would have passed against the old `localStorage` store too, which is exactly the defect (annotations under `draazyLeadNotes:<ownerDigits>` were per-browser, so an owner's phone and laptop kept disjoint CRMs). | **"Request more photos" carries a signal between two people, and both terminal decisions reach the buyer.** The retired mock twin read `draazyPhotoReq:<ownerDigits>` back out of the asking browser's own `localStorage`, and seeded that same key by hand for the owner half — so the two tests shared no state and the hand-off itself was never exercised. The decline path is new coverage, not a port: `declined` shipped in V118 with no browser test, which is how the decision body came to be omitted from the client and broke the live flow — the mock asserted a Decline button *existed* and never pressed it. | **The `formDetails` allowlist agrees across the two languages that own it.** `formDetails` is free-form JSONB in the spec, so the key set is hand-written twice — `DETAIL_KEYS` in `lib/listingFormDetails.js` decides what leaves the browser, `TEXT`/`FLAGS`/`ARRAYS` in `ListingFormDetails.java` decides what the server keeps — and a key in one table only fails **silently in both directions**: missing from the JS set the answer is dropped before the request is built (no 422, no log, no red test — the client censors itself, which is exactly how `transactionType` was collected on both wizards and stored by neither), and missing from the Java set the whole submit 422s on a constraint whose message names no field at all. Neither file is wrong on its own, which is why nothing could catch it until the two were compared. `check:enums` now reads the Java tables with a line scanner and asserts set equality against the exported JS set, with a blindness guard (`> 40` keys) so a refactor that defeats the scanner goes red rather than vacuously green. Mutation-checked by deleting `rentMaintMode` from the Java table: *"client has 'rentMaintMode' and the contract does not"* | API contract | frontend/scripts/check-enum-vocabulary.mjs, backend `ListingFormDetailsTest` | ✅ |
